a directory that takes the push workers or the sink fails fast (`DirectPushWiringTests`); over HTTP a forward for a recipient without a live session is posted to its endpoint and removed; a failing endpoint leaves its message for pickup and is never posted again while a second recipient is pushed; a slow endpoint under the deadline delivers, a tarpitting one is given up at the deadline; a revoke-and-purge before the take leaves nothing to post and one during the POST lets it complete; a message that expires before its take is never posted, a delayed one is posted only once due, an acknowledged pickup before the take leaves nothing to post, and a pickup during a held POST gets nothing; live first, then direct once the session is gone, nothing delivered twice; after a restart nothing attempted is posted again (`DirectPushEndToEndTests`). |
| FR-PICK-10 | MUST | **Limits (D7).** Per-grant quotas `max_queue_messages` and `max_queue_bytes` (`MaxQueueMessages`, default 100; `MaxQueueBytes`, default 8 MiB) are host-configurable and bound the grant's **total retained storage**: queued, leased (delivered-not-acknowledged, FR-PICK-04), live-delivered-not-acknowledged (FR-PICK-08) and not-yet-due payloads all count against the same budget — a live push never bypasses it. The in-memory store also bounds all grants together (`MaxStoreMessages`, default 5 000; `MaxStoreBytes`, default 128 MiB); its defaults are modest on purpose (owner decision of 2026-11-12: the in-memory stores are for samples, tests and small hosts; worst-case memory at the defaults is about 170 MiB — the byte bound plus about 8 KiB of keys per record). Under open enrolment self-made grants can use up the store-wide bounds, after which new messages are refused for everyone while what is held stays deliverable (a stated availability limit, like W-2). Capacity is reserved by admitting the payload to the store before any push; a payload that cannot be admitted is not pushed. A record that is expired when a message arrives never keeps it out: expired records are swept from the arriving grant, and from the whole store when the store-wide bound would otherwise refuse. `DidCommOptions.MaxReceiveBytes` applies to every retained payload (nothing larger is admitted), and so does the single-item delivery bound of FR-PICK-03. A payload exceeding a quota is dropped and logged (counts and enum names only); the transport result stays 202 (no sender-side oracle) and the recipient observes the cap through `status.total_bytes` for what is due — not-yet-due payloads (FR-ROUTE-10) count against the quota without being reported (FR-PICK-02), so a sender that asks for long delays could use a recipient's quota up unseen until they come due: the mediator MUST bound the delay it honours, and does with the FR-ROUTE-10 cap — under `AddMediator()` a sender can keep a recipient's quota occupied by messages the recipient cannot see for at most `MaxForwardDelay` (1 hour by default) after it stops sending (owner-accepted residual). **Mailbox age limit** (owner decision of 2026-11-12): `MessagePickupOptions.MaxMessageAge` (default **30 days**; `null` turns it off; otherwise greater than zero) bounds how long the queue keeps any message, counted from when the queue accepted it, on the mediator's clock (`DidCommOptions.Clock`, no skew). The queue folds it into the record's `QueuedMessage.ExpiresAt` at admission — the earlier of the forward's own expiry and `ReceivedAt + MaxMessageAge`, saturating at `DateTimeOffset.MaxValue` — so every expiry path (lease selection, `status`, the per-grant and store-full sweeps, `PurgeExpiredAsync`) applies it to waiting, leased and passed-over records alike, by time only, and a custom `IMessageStore` needs no change for it (what a custom store must add for live pushes is the arrival sequence, FR-PICK-09). The limit in force at admission is fixed on the record: lowering it later, or restarting with another value over a persistent store, does not shorten what is already held. **A recipient that stays offline longer than the limit loses those messages silently** — they are never delivered, and neither the sender nor the recipient is told; set the limit to the longest absence your recipients must survive. Under `AddMediator()` the limit, when set, must be longer than `MaxForwardDelay` (refused otherwise: a delayed message would age out before it is due), and the mediator's hosted service removes expired and aged-out records every `PurgeInterval` (default 5 minutes; 1 second to 24 hours); expired and aged-out records are never delivered whether or not the purge has run. `recipient_did`, attachment ids and `message_id_list` entries are bounded by FR-API-09 (2048; never echoed in error text or logs); the length of `message_id_list` is bounded by a host-configurable `max_ack_ids` (`MaxAckIds`; default: the delivery batch maximum × 10), applied from the list's length before any entry is decoded (and, for a message as it arrives off the wire, before anything is allocated per entry); sessions per DID are bounded (FR-RR-03). **Removed DIDs and revoked grants** (owner decision of 2026-11-12): the mediation registry raises no change notification; registration is checked when messages are delivered (FR-PICK-03, FR-PICK-09), and the store offers `PurgeRecipientAsync` and `PurgeGrantAsync`. A host ends a peer's mediation with `MessagePickupQueue.RevokeAndPurgeAsync(peerDid)`: it revokes the grant (`IMediationStore.RevokeAsync`), then removes everything held under it (`IMessageStore.PurgeGrantAsync`), and returns `GrantRevocation(Revoked, Purged)`; a value that is not a bare DID is refused without being quoted, store faults propagate (a host API), and calling it again after a fault purges what is left. **Race (L-057):** a forward admitted before the revoke can store its record after the purge; `DeliverAsync`'s second registration lookup (FR-PICK-09) then removes exactly that record — unless that lookup, or the removal, fails, in which case the record stays until it expires or ages out. A live push from the mediator's push workers takes its record from the store and checks its registration before sending (FR-PICK-09), so it never sends a record the purge removed before the push took it. Residuals: a live delivery or a push that has taken its record completes; if the same peer is granted mediation again while the call runs, what was stored for the new grant before the purge is removed too (deny the peer in the policy before revoking). A host that revokes through `IMediationStore.RevokeAsync` alone calls `PurgeGrantAsync` itself; until then the revoked grant's leftovers are retained and count against the store-wide bounds — not delivered while the peer holds no grant, and delivered again if the same peer is granted mediation again (records are kept under the peer's DID). Faults of the stores, the session registry or the session never leave the role: a request gets no reply (leases it took are released), a delivery is reported `Faulted`; only cancellation of the caller's token propagates. Admission order (#100): the size bound first (an over-size payload is `PayloadTooLarge` and is never parsed), then the encrypted-form check (FR-PICK-03), then the expiry and the registration lookup, so a refused payload costs no mediation-store call and no quota. | A flood past `max_queue_bytes` stays within the bound and still returns 202; a live session that reads a stream of N pushes without acknowledging, then disconnects, never has more than `max_queue_bytes` / `max_queue_messages` retained at any instant — before the pushes, during, and after the disconnect releases the leases (no requeue copy is made) — and pushes beyond the budget are dropped (logged), not retained; a `message_id_list` one entry past `max_ack_ids` is rejected as malformed with the count as the reason even when the extra entry is itself invalid, and a 10^6-entry list is refused without per-entry allocation; a cap + 1 id → malformed, no echo; an expired record of another grant does not keep a message out of a full store; each collaborator faulting on each path that reads it leaves no exception, no half-applied change and no stuck lease. Mailbox age limit: 30 days by default, written onto the record; one tick before the limit a message is counted and deliverable, at the limit it is not counted, not leased, not delivered (`status` and `delivery-request` see nothing) and `PurgeExpiredAsync` removes it; the store-full sweep frees another grant's aged records; a leased record, a record under a younger live lease and a passed-over record age out, and a later acknowledgement is ignored; with the limit off a message never ages; the stored expiry is the earlier of the forward's expiry and the limit, while "expired at admission" is judged on the forward's expiry alone; the limit saturates near `DateTimeOffset.MaxValue` and is read once when the queue is built (`PickupMessageAgeTests`); a limit not longer than the delay cap is refused, one tick longer or off is accepted (`MediatorOptionsTests`). `RevokeAndPurgeAsync`: revokes the grant and removes everything held under it, leaves other grants alone and is idempotent; a peer with no grant revokes nothing and purges what was left under its key; a value that is not a bare DID is refused before any store is called and not quoted; a mediation-store fault propagates and purges nothing, a message-store fault propagates and a retry purges what is left; the outcome and the log line name no DID (`PickupRevokeAndPurgeTests`). |
| FR-PICK-11 | MUST | **Recipient role (client).** Provide `PickupClient` (package and namespace `DidComm.Mediator.Client`, registered with `MediationClient` by `AddMediatorClient()` and set up the same way, FR-MED-09; built without DI it works over HTTP only): `GetStatusAsync`; `FetchAsync` (sends `delivery-request`, unpacks each attachment with the recipient's own client, and acknowledges only the attachments that unpacked — a failed unpack is reported to the caller and left on the queue, never silently acknowledged); `AckAsync` (returns `Task<PickupStatus?>`: the mediator's own `status` answering the last message, or `null` when it sent none, which Message Pickup 3.0 allows (L193: "The `mediator` **SHOULD** send an updated `status` message reflecting the changes to the queue.") and 4.0 always does (L294 drops that sentence); it never sends a `status-request` of its own, because one asked for separately is not ordered after an acknowledgement nothing answered; a message that could not be sent is an exception, never `null`; over a WebSocket a 3.0 status is waited for half of `RequestTimeout` once the message is sent, never past its `RequestTimeout`; #98, owner decision OD-6 on #100, 2026-11-21); `SetLiveDeliveryAsync` (WebSocket only; refused with `InvalidOperationException` over HTTP before anything is sent); `ReceiveLiveAsync` (acknowledges each item after the consumer has taken it). **As built (#96):** `FetchAsync` takes a `limit` of 1 to 100 and reads at most that many attachments, each on its own (what the mediator sends beyond it is not opened or acknowledged and stays queued; an attachment without a usable id is skipped; a delivery in which no message has a usable id is a `MediationProtocolException`); it returns a `PickupBatch` (`Items` in delivery order — each a `PickupItem` with the mediator's opaque `Id`, either `Unpacked` or `Error`, and for a failed item `Inconclusive` —, `Drained`, and `Status` when the mediator answered with one) and acknowledges what unpacked before it returns, so the caller then holds the only copy; cancelling before that acknowledgement leaves everything queued, and a failed acknowledgement still returns the batch; over HTTP a `messages-received` left without a status (an empty 202, as the Identus mediator answers; Message Pickup 3.0 makes that status a SHOULD) is not a failed acknowledgement and is not logged as one (#98). An optional `recipientDid` filter is sent and enforced: a message is that DID's only when one of that DID's own keys opened it (the other recipients an envelope lists are not evidence: whoever encrypts a layer writes that list); any other is a failed item (`MediationProtocolException`), not acknowledged. So a message encrypted to two of the host's DIDs, which opens with the key its envelope lists first among those held, is accepted without a filter or under that first DID's filter; under the second DID's filter it is a failed item "for another of your DIDs" (owner decision on #88, 2026-11-14). Such an item is the host's to settle: the host takes it with a fetch without the filter, or discards it with `AckAsync`, and does not leave it queued; while it is queued it is opened again at every delivery under the filter and stays in front of what came after it, and where a delivery brings more than sixteen messages, sixteen such messages that are slow to open keep the ones behind them from their turn at every fetch, so that those come back inconclusive each time (#111, the coordinator's call). All the messages of one delivery — a fetch, a live drain round or a push — are opened under one budget, `MediatorClientOptions.UnpackTimeout` (20 s by default, a third of this library's 60 s `LeaseTimeout`; above 0, at most 10 minutes), up to sixteen at the same time (#111, owner decision G, "Open 16 at once (Recommended)"), and a message has no time limit of its own: it runs until it opens, fails or the delivery ends (#111, owner decision E, "Remove per-message limits (Recommended)"). A message starts only while at least a quarter of the budget is left; one whose turn comes later, or that has no outcome when the budget ends, is a failed item with a `TimeoutException` naming at most the request id ("ran out before its turn came", "ran out while it was being opened"), not acknowledged, and the rest of the delivery is handled as usual. A delivery whose messages never open therefore lasts its whole budget, still inside the lease. The budget stays below the mediator's lease, so while the mediator still sets such a message aside the next fetch reaches what is queued behind it; the client never discards a failed item on its own (the host discards one with `AckAsync`, and only one that is not inconclusive: below). Every unpack runs on a thread-pool work item of its own, so a fetch or a stream is not held on a resolver's thread and the budget decides the outcome even when a DID or secrets resolver blocks its thread; blocking host code is not bounded in time (the thread and its work stay the host's until it returns, and the unpack keeps its reservation meanwhile), and such a resolver holds up to sixteen thread-pool threads per delivery (one host call per work item, sixteen opened at once). The container's disposal ends a delivery being opened at once with `ObjectDisposedException` and cancels the token the host code was given. A message that never opens stays queued and counts against the recipient's mailbox quota at the mediator (this library's: `MessagePickupOptions.MaxQueueMessages`, 100) until the host discards it or it ages out (30 days by default); while such messages fill the quota, the mediator accepts no further message for that mailbox, so a host discards the ones it has given up on. **Slow senders (#111, built):** the client remembers, per (mediator, mailbox), a delivered message whose host call ran (its code started) and did not open, in two cases only (owner decision C on #111, "Every failure, fair to late starts (Recommended)"): it failed on its own while its delivery was not being ended, whatever the duration and however late it started; or the budget's own timer ended the delivery, the message had no outcome yet, and its code had started within the start allowance of the budget's start (an eighth of the budget, 2.5 s by default; this rule for the budget's end, and the allowance, are the coordinator's call, not the owner's). A host call waits a little for a thread before its code runs, so an honest message whose code started within the allowance and has not opened when the budget ends is remembered too, also when a busy thread pool cost it part of that allowance (R-15). Nothing else is remembered: not a message whose code started later than the allowance (it is opened again at the next delivery, where it starts earlier), one still running when the caller cancelled or the container shut down, one refused for capacity, one held back, a malformed one, or one that did not start; and a message still inside its pause gets no further strike, so deliveries of it opened at the same time count once (RT1B-2). Whether a failure is the message's own or the end of its delivery is decided by the state of the delivery's, the caller's and the container's tokens when the failure is observed, never by the exception's type: host code may report a cancellation as a failure of its own (the coordinator's call; the same rule decides a live stream's requests and the channel's sends and endpoint lookups, where an answer that had already won still holds). A failed item says whether this delivery counted against its message: `PickupItem.Inconclusive` (the one public member #111 adds, in a package not yet published; the coordinator's call) is true when the message did not open and this delivery did not count against it: its turn did not come before the budget ran out (no call was created for it, the start rule turned it away, or its call's code had not started; "before its turn came"); it was refused for capacity ("unpack capacity exhausted"); or its code started later than the start allowance and it was still being opened when the budget ended ("while it was being opened"). Within one delivery these are exactly the failed messages the memory does not strike: the flag and the memory are one decision. When two deliveries of the same message overlap, each outcome says whether that delivery gave the message the whole budget, and the message is counted once. It is false for every other item: one that opened, one that failed on its own, one that had the whole budget and did not open, one held back, one that is not a message this client opens, and one a `recipientDid` filter turned away. A host does not discard an item whose `Inconclusive` is true: it is delivered again and started again then, once what was in its way is gone. False alone is not a reason to discard either: when the flag is false the delivery counted against the message, the client tries it again after its pause, and giving up on it is the host's decision. A message a `recipientDid` filter turned away is a case of its own: it opened, so its flag is false and it is not remembered, and it is the host's to settle as above. When a fetch returns only inconclusive items the host waits before it fetches again (for the lease at a mediator that sets delivered messages aside; for the lookups still running otherwise): a fetch that brings only refused items takes no time and is not drained, so a fetch-until-drained loop would spin. A live stream is not in that position: a round that brings back only items of deliveries its drain took ends the drain, and such items come again at the next cycle. Why the flag exists: the failures for lack of time are all `TimeoutException` and differ only in their English text, and a host that discarded every failed item also discarded messages that had not been given their chance. The key is a fixed-size hash of the mediator DID, the mailbox DID and the mediator's attachment id (compared exactly); nothing a sender writes decides a write. When a remembered message is delivered again it is not opened: it is a failed item at once (`TimeoutException`, "it did not open when it was delivered before"), runs no host code, takes no budget and is not acknowledged, for a pause of 30 × `UnpackTimeout` (10 minutes) doubling to 256 × (about 85 minutes); a message that opens is forgotten. A clock reading below the one a message was last struck at counts as due, so after the host clock steps back the message is opened once more. If it fails again, that is the same slow episode: no strike is added, and it is held back for the same pause again, counted from that failure, not for the next, doubled pause. So a step back can make a message due later than without it (in the measured case half a minute: minute 30.5 instead of minute 30), and each further step back costs one more opening. A message that failed on its own is held back for its first pause, an honest message that failed only for a passing reason (its sender's DID host briefly down) included: it comes back after 10 minutes, not at the next fetch. A fetch-until-drained loop reaches the message behind N ≤ 256 that never open, on the condition that the lookups of one delivery have ended when the next one starts (a delivery that starts earlier refuses what the reservations do not leave free, which takes no time, and those messages wait for their next delivery, at most one lease): with ℓ the messages each fetch gets and B the budget, learning them once takes L = ⌈N/ℓ⌉·B for ℓ ≤ 16 and L = ⌈ℓ/16⌉ · (⌈N/ℓ⌉·B + one lease) above 16 (a delivery starts at most 16); the message is reached within L when L is at most the first pause, otherwise within 2 · ⌈log2(L / first pause)⌉ · L (checked by measurement, not proven: of 96 cells measured at a simulated mediator that honours the limit asked for, 66 are exactly at the bound, 30 are below it and none is above). At this library's mediator's defaults (ten per delivery, a quota of 100, a 60 s lease), behind 99 messages that never open the message is returned after 200 s, ten fetches of one budget each; fetched with `limit` 4, after 500 s. A live stream, measured at a simulated mediator that sets delivered messages aside (not this library's), at the default `LivePollInterval`, yields it after 380 s, in its fourth cycle, at a 60 s lease; at a 10 s lease after 1380 s, in cycle 18, the messages learned first being opened a second time from 640 s on. A host that discards a failed item unless it is inconclusive receives the message behind 99 that never open after 200 s at `limit` 10, 140 s at 16 and 380 s at 100 (there it is first handed over six times as inconclusive), and behind 300 after 600 s, 380 s and 380 s (a 60 s lease, a simulated mediator that honours the limit asked for); a host that discards every failure loses a message that was only inconclusive. Every host unpack call is reserved before it starts and holds its reservation until it ends, whoever waits for it and also after its delivery has ended: at most 16 per sender (the DID subject of the outer authcrypt envelope's `skid`; that header only picks the bound, it decides nothing about what is opened; raised with owner decision G on #111, "Open 16 at once (Recommended)"; the bound is as many as one delivery opens at once, and the two numbers change together), 16 per mailbox for messages whose outer envelope names no sender, and 256 per container (owner decision D on #111, "Raise to 256, document (Recommended)"); a message beyond a bound fails at once ("unpack capacity exhausted"), is not remembered and is not acknowledged. So one sender's stalled lookups do not refuse another sender's message within a mailbox, and across mailboxes up to that bound: one sender whose lookups do not return is held to sixteen host calls, its further messages are refused at once (inconclusive), and with thirty of its messages in front another sender's message behind them is returned after 40 s at `limit` 10 and after 80 s at `limit` 4 (when that sender's lookups honour their token, each of the thirty is opened once and none is refused, and the message is returned after 60 s and 160 s). The bounds do not cap the lookups of one DID: per mailbox, up to 16 messages naming it and 16 naming no sender can be resolving it at once; a sender's bound is counted by the DID subject exactly as the envelope writes it, which the client does not normalise, and the client does not limit the host's resolver per DID: a limit per DID, where a host needs one, belongs in its resolver (QA2-2). Every lookup is handed the delivery's token; a lookup is host code, should watch the token it is handed, and may outlive its delivery. The memory holds at most 256 messages per (mediator, mailbox) and 256 such pairs, the least recently seen going first, in process only. **What remains (documented, not fixed):** a reservation is held until its lookup ends, so lookups that do not honour cancellation keep theirs, and the per-sender bound limits one sender's share of a mailbox, not the sum over senders: the container's 256 is sixteen partitions at their bound of sixteen, in one mailbox or across sixteen, and while all are held every delivered message fails at once for every tenant; with lookups that do honour their tokens the same count applies while they run, so another mailbox's message is refused for the container, not remembered, and comes back (R-1); messages naming no sender share one bound of 16 per mailbox (R-2); with no time limit per message, sixteen messages that never open at the front of a delivery hold every worker for one budget, and those behind them in the same delivery run out of time before their turn (inconclusive) and get it in the next delivery, where the sixteen are held back — only a delivery of more than sixteen has messages behind, and this library's mediator delivers at most ten per fetch at its defaults (R-3); with more than 256 messages that never open queued in front (a mediator whose quota is above this library's 100), the message behind is reached only once the host discards the failures it is handed with `AckAsync`, never an inconclusive one, and more than 256 pairs make remembered messages be opened and learned again (R-4); the memory lives in the process and keeps a message for at most its longest pause, about 85 minutes, so a delivery that comes later than that after the one before, or to a new process, finds nothing remembered: with sixteen or more that never open in front in one delivery the message behind them is then not returned; a host runs its loop again soon in the same process (measured with runs five minutes apart: returned in the second run) or discards the failed items that are not inconclusive (measured: it then receives the message also with a new process for every fetch) (R-5; it takes a mediator that delivers more than sixteen per fetch, and this library's mediator delivers at most ten per fetch at its defaults); a mediator that does not set delivered messages aside still needs the host to discard what keeps failing with `AckAsync` (never an inconclusive item), and its fetches over remembered messages take no time (R-6); a message a `recipientDid` filter turned away opened (its flag is false), is not remembered and is not acknowledged, and is the host's to settle with a fetch without the filter or with `AckAsync`; left queued, it is opened again at every delivery under the filter and stays in front of what came after it (R-7); up to 256 reservations can be held by host code that does not return, and a resolver that blocks its thread holds a thread-pool thread per lookup, up to sixteen per delivery; with such a resolver messages can start later than the start allowance, and are then not remembered at the budget's end (they are inconclusive and tried again), and the budget's own timer can be delayed (R-8); a message that never opens and whose code starts later than the start allowance in every delivery is inconclusive every time and is never remembered: that is pinned over ten deliveries by a test that makes the late starts by moving the clock, and on a real clock they happen with a host resolver that blocks its thread on a small thread pool; without a filter, or with a host that settles what a filter turned away, no way was found for a message to stay inconclusive delivery after delivery (reasoning only, on the assumption that the mediator delivers oldest first); under a `recipientDid` filter with messages it turned away left queued, a message behind sixteen of them that are slow to open does stay inconclusive at every fetch, which takes all of a host that fetches under a filter and never without one, messages encrypted for another DID whose keys the host holds, and deliveries of more than sixteen messages (not this library's mediator at its defaults); what a host does about it is to settle what the filter turned away (R-14; the coordinator's call); R-9 is not used; the live stream learns per cycle in proportion to the lease (R-10); while a live stream's cycle requests all fail, each push that brings something new and each accepted acknowledgement lets its give-up point go, so the stream does not end as long as one of them comes before each give-up point, and its cycle requests stay at one per back-off, at most one per 30 s; a host that wants the stream to end cancels the enumeration (R-11; owner decisions on #111: B1, "Push keeps the wait (Recommended)", as refined by F, "Only cycle success resets (Recommended)"); a drain that outlasts the lease ends on a round of messages it already handled, so what is behind them waits one more `LivePollInterval`, nothing lost (R-12; ours, not an owner decision: a 3.0 `delivery-request` has no offset); a lookup that has been cancelled keeps its place until it ends, and if the next delivery arrives before that, messages of the same sender (or, without a named sender, of that mailbox) beyond the places still free are refused at once, are inconclusive, and come again, other senders' messages not being affected — rare with a lookup that ends as soon as it is cancelled, the usual outcome with one that takes even a millisecond to end when a delivery follows immediately; a host call that never got a thread keeps its place as well, until the thread pool reaches its cancelled work item (R-13); and host code (a resolver, a transport) that watches a different token than the one it is handed, and reports that token's cancellation as a failure, has that failure taken as the message's or the request's own (R-16). `AckAsync` discards items by id (1 to 10 000, 100 per message). `ReceiveLiveAsync` is refused before anything is sent when the route is not a WebSocket; it turns live delivery on, drains what is queued, takes what is pushed (each delivery opened whole, under the budget, before its first item is yielded), and repeats the live-on and the drain one `LivePollInterval` after each drain ends — a started cycle runs its drain to its end before the next is armed, however slowly the mediator answers (PR #112 review R1-1; `PickupClientLiveSchedulingTests`); a failed step is retried after 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, then 30 s (a failed acknowledgement is sent again first, and a cycle follows once it is accepted), and the stream gives up at a retryable failure that comes more than `RequestTimeout` + 30 s after the first failure since the give-up point was last let go (60 s in all at the defaults; ADV5-2); a drain that ends and a drain round that brings something new, and only these two, restart the back-off and let the give-up point go, while a push that brings something new and an acknowledgement the mediator accepted let the give-up point go but keep the back-off (#111, owner decisions B1, "Push keeps the wait (Recommended)", and F, "Only cycle success resets (Recommended)"); a request that fails once the caller's token is cancelled ends the stream as cancelled, whatever it reported; a drain also ends at a round that brings nothing new: everything in it is held, or came in a delivery this drain took, and what a drain took is remembered until a drain ends, not until the next cycle (at most 990 ids), a failed step not ending the drain (#111) — the way a delayed message arrives and a dropped connection recovers (FR-PICK-08). Once a cycle is draining, its drain rounds and the pushes take turns, a drain round first (a push that waits stays leased to the session, so nothing is lost): a steady flow of pushes that are slow to open cannot keep the drain from running, nor a drain that keeps meeting slow messages the pushes. The cycle, the back-off, the acknowledgement flush and the give-up point are each a timer the stream sets itself, and nothing is decided by comparing clock readings, so a host clock that steps back or forward costs nothing: no early cycle and no postponement (#111). "Taken" means the consumer asks for the next item: when the enumeration ends — `break`, an exception out of the loop body, cancellation — the last item yielded is not acknowledged and comes back (at-least-once; `AckAsync(item.Id)` discards it), and live delivery is turned off. Failed items are yielded with their error, never acknowledged. Delivery is at-least-once: a repeat is acknowledged and not returned again while the client remembers it (`DedupeCapacity`, 4096 by default — with 0 a fetch still returns a message that comes twice in one delivery once, while a live stream yields it twice; keyed by the mailbox read — the `from` of the fetch or stream — with the sender, decrypting DID and message id, or with the bytes of an anonymous message, so a copy relayed into another of the host's mailboxes never makes the first mailbox's copy a repeat). An in-thread `e.m.live-mode-not-supported` problem report surfaces as a typed protocol exception carrying the code (`MediationProtocolException.ProblemCode`). A rewrapped (double-packaged) delivery unwraps at the recipient (FR-ROUTE-06, receiver side). Every request and acknowledgement is timestamped per FR-RR-09, and acknowledgements reuse the session socket (FR-RR-07 affinity). **Message Pickup 4.0 (#100):** the client speaks 4.0 or 3.0 per mediator, chosen the way the Coordinate Mediation version is (FR-MED-09): `MediatorClientOptions.MessagePickupVersion` (`Negotiate`, the default; `V4`; `V3`) pins it, else one Discover Features message with two queries, `https://didcomm.org/message-pickup/4.*` then `https://didcomm.org/messagepickup/3.*`, under the same role rule (owner decision 2026-11-18, #99); 4.0 wins; neither → `MediationNotSupportedException`; no answer → `TimeoutException`; the choice is remembered per mediator for an hour (at most 64), and a live stream keeps the version it started with. Under 4.0: `delivery-request` carries `message_count_limit`; a delivery without an `attachments` array, or with more attachments than asked for, is refused whole as `MediationProtocolException` before anything in it is opened (4.0 L255, L259); an empty delivery is a drained fetch with no `PickupBatch.Status`; a `status` is no answer to a `delivery-request`; `messages-received` goes one-way, without `return_route` (4.0 L35) and with nothing awaited — over a WebSocket it rides the session socket (FR-RR-07) and is timestamped by FR-RR-09's PIURI clause — so `AckAsync` returns `null` (OD-6), and nothing confirms the acknowledgement, nor the one a 4.0 `FetchAsync` sends. Closing the connection right after either relies on the close (owner decisions OD-7 and OD-8, 2026-11-21): disposing the container waits, up to `WebSocketTransportOptions.SendTimeout`, for the mediator to answer the transport's close (FR-TRN-11), which this library's mediator does only after it has handled what it received before the close; and this library's mediator finishes a message it received in full when the connection drops behind it, for at most 5 s (FR-TRN-14). An abrupt drop before the message is complete, or a mediator slower than those bounds (or one that drops the work of a closed connection), can still mean the messages come back (at-least-once). Cookbook section EE and sample 11 acknowledge, then dispose, with no polling; `PickupStatus` keeps epoch seconds (4.0's milliseconds rounded down) and `LongestWaitedSeconds` is null; a thread-less 4.0 `delivery` only ever reaches a live stream (owner answer "2", #98), and a push of the other version is not taken. **In both versions (#100):** a `status` answering a filtered request must repeat the `recipient_did` (3.0 L116, 4.0 L168 MUST): an absent echo is refused like a wrong one, and the echo of a `status` or a `delivery` must be "the matching value" itself — compared exactly as DIDs are, so a DID URL or another spelling of the DID asked about is refused (ADV3-2; before, the two were compared as DID subjects); a problem report answers a request only through its `pthid` (FR-MED-04, ADV3-1); and a delivered attachment that is not a DIDComm message in encrypted form (FR-PICK-03's rule, judged on its outermost layer before any unpack) is a failed item, not acknowledged (owner decision 2026-11-20, "OK, as described"). | Fetch → unpack → ack → `status` 0 over HTTP (`PickupClientHttpEndToEndTests.Enrol_send_fetch_and_acknowledge_then_the_status_is_zero`, `PickupClientAcceptanceQa2Tests`); live mode over WS: queued messages by the drain, later ones by push, each acknowledged when the next is asked for, live delivery off at the end (`PickupClientWebSocketEndToEndTests`, `PickupClientAcceptanceQa2Tests`); a batch with one undecryptable attachment returns the good ones and leaves the bad one queued (`PickupClientHttpEndToEndTests.A_message_Bob_cannot_open_is_a_failure_never_acknowledged_and_still_counted`, `PickupClientAcceptanceQa2Tests.Partial_unpack_…`, `PickupClientFetchTests`); HTTP `SetLiveDeliveryAsync` throws before any send (`PickupClientHttpEndToEndTests.Live_delivery_over_HTTP_is_refused_with_no_request_reaching_the_mediator`, `PickupClientAckTests`). Also: the limit, the filter, repeats, cancellation before and after the acknowledgement, and a fetch-until-drained loop past more failures than its limit (`PickupClientFetchTests`, `PickupClientFetchAndRewrapQa2Tests`, `PickupClientDedupeQa2Tests`); the live stream's acknowledgement on the next `MoveNextAsync` and at its end, its cycle, back-off and failures (`PickupClientLiveStreamTests`, `PickupClientLiveCycleTests`, `PickupClientLiveRobustnessQa2Tests`); a delayed message reaches a live stream through its periodic cycle (`PickupClientWebSocketEndToEndTests.A_delayed_message_is_picked_up_by_the_periodic_cycle_on_the_clients_clock`, `PickupClientDelayedQa2Tests`); clock skew (`PickupClientClockSkewQa2Tests`). The filter accepts only what the filter DID's own key opened, whatever the envelope lists, and the two-DID case fails under the second DID's filter, is accepted under the first's or unfiltered and is discarded with `AckAsync` (`PickupClientFetchTests`, `PickupClientQa2Tests.Q2_1_…`, `PickupClientAdv1Tests`); a message returned from one mailbox is not a repeat in another (`DuplicateFilterTests`, `PickupClientFetchTests`, `PickupClientAdv1Tests`, `PickupClientDedupeQa2Tests`); one budget per delivery, sixteen opened at once with no time limit per message, the start rule, the reservations per sender, per mailbox and per container, and the memory of messages that did not open (`DeliveredMessageUnpackerTests`, `DeliveredUnpackAdv2Tests`, `DeliveredUnpackSlowSenderTests`, `DeliveredUnpackNoPerMessageLimitTests`, `DeliveredUnpackSixteenAtOnceTests`, `SlowMemoryTests`, `SlowMemoryClockStepTests`, `UnpackReservationsTests`, `SenderPartitionTests`), in a fetch and in the live stream, where a delivery is opened whole before its first item (`PickupClientFetchTests`, `PickupClientLiveStreamTests`); the default budget well inside the default lease (`MediatorClientOptionsTests`); against this library's mediator, a fetch-until-drained loop gets past messages the budget gave up on, and a lease that runs out inside the budget hands the messages to the next fetch while the late acknowledgement is still accepted (`PickupClientBudgetLeaseTests`). Fix round 3: drain rounds and pushes take turns under a steady flow of slow pushes — a delayed message still arrives, end to end against this library's mediator too — and under a drain that keeps meeting slow messages; a host clock stepped back an hour postpones neither the next cycle, a back-off nor the acknowledgement flush (`PickupClientLiveFix3Tests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv3Tests`); unpacks never run on the caller's stack, a resolver that blocks its thread is decided at the budget and keeps its reservation, and the container's disposal ends a fetch or a live stream opening a delivery at once, cancelling the host code's tokens (`DeliveredUnpackFix3Tests`, `DeliveredUnpackAdv3Tests`, `MediatorClientChannelAdv3Tests`). #111: against this library's mediator, which delivers at most ten messages per fetch at its defaults, a fetch-until-drained loop reaches the message behind 1 to 99 messages that never open within the bound at `limit` 4 and 10 and leases of 60 and 10 s, each cell's measured time pinned (99 in front: 500 s at `limit` 4, 200 s at 10); one sender whose lookups ignore their token is held to its sixteen reservations while another sender's message is returned after 40 s at `limit` 10 and 80 s at `limit` 4, beside the same thirty when its lookups honour their token (60 s and 160 s); and an honest slow sender's message behind junk naming its key reaches the application within the bound (`PickupClientSlowSenderAcceptanceTests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv3Tests.ADV3_6_…`); the live stream's version of that matrix, each cell pinned (`PickupClientLiveSlowSenderTests`); the fetch loop's 96 measured cells against the bound, and a fetch that arrives while the lookups of the one before are still ending (`SlowSenderFetchMatrixTests`, `DeliveredUnpackNextDeliveryRt3Tests`); a cancellation told from a failure by the tokens' state in the unpacker, the fetch, the stream and the channel, and an answer that had won holding (`DeliveredUnpackTokenStateTests`, `PickupClientFetchTokenStateTests`, `LiveStreamCancelledStepTests`, `MediatorClientChannelTokenStateTests`, `MediatorClientChannelTokenStateRt3Tests`); a request decided where its call's end is, so that an answer arriving once the call has been ended is not taken (`MediatorClientChannelAnswerAndSendRt4Tests`, `MediatorClientChannelEndDecidedTests`); the memory under a clock that steps back (`SlowMemoryClockStepTests`, `SlowMemoryRt2BTests`); the stream's scheduling by its own timers and its back-off (`PickupClientLiveSchedulingTests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv4Tests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv5Tests`, `LiveStreamRt2CTests`, `LiveStreamBackoffRt3Tests`); the flag: which outcomes are inconclusive, that the flag says what the memory says, that a fetch and a live stream pass it on, and what a host that discards a failed item unless it is inconclusive receives (`DeliveredUnpackInconclusiveTests`, `InconclusiveMatchesTheMemoryTests`, `PickupItemInconclusiveTests`, `SlowSenderDiscardingHostRt3Tests`, `LiveStreamDiscardingHostTests`, `InconclusiveLateStartRt4Tests`); under a `recipientDid` filter, a host that leaves queued what the filter turned away does not receive the message behind sixteen such messages (inconclusive at every fetch, 120 times in two simulated hours), while a host that discards unless inconclusive, one that takes them with a fetch without the filter, and one that uses no filter each receive it (after 60 s; after 120 s when it takes them at the next delivery), and the failure's text names the two ways to settle such a message (`InconclusiveUnderAFilterRt4Tests`, `PickupItemInconclusiveTests`); two overlapping deliveries of one message (`InconclusiveOverlappingDeliveriesRt4Tests`); the residuals' pins (`ContainerSharingRt1ATests`, `UnpackReservationsSixteenRt4Tests`, `SlowSenderSpacedDeliveriesRt3Tests`, `DeliveredUnpackTokenStateRt3Tests`, `InconclusiveBusyPoolRt4Tests`). The reproductions #96 deferred to #111 now pass and pin the fixes (`DeliveredUnpackAdv3Tests` ADV3-2 and ADV3-3, `DeliveredUnpackAdv4Tests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv4Tests`, `PickupClientLiveAdv5Tests`, the interop `PickupClientLiveAdv3Tests` ADV3-6), and ADV2-2's per-sender bound and its residuals are pinned (`DeliveredUnpackAdv2Tests`). 4.0 (#100): negotiation table, query order, pin and cache (`PickupClientNegotiationTests`, `PickupV4NegotiationEndToEndTests`); every call under 4.0 against fakes (`PickupClientV4Tests`, `PickupClientStrictnessTests`, `MediatorClientChannelV4Tests`) and against the real 4.0 role over HTTP and WebSocket (`PickupV4WireEndToEndTests`, `PickupV4MailboxEndToEndTests`, `PickupV4ThreadCaseEndToEndTests`); the live stream under 4.0, including a lease that runs out with the connection open and an idle socket that outlasts many `MessageReceiveTimeout`s on both sides (`PickupClientLiveV4Tests`, `PickupV4LiveEndToEndTests`). OD-6: `AckAsync` returns only the mediator's own answer and sends no `status-request` — under 4.0 `null` whatever its connection does, with the acknowledgement held at the store and applied after; under 3.0 a status whose `thid` is its own `messages-received`, or `null`, never an earlier chunk's (`PickupV4AckOrderEndToEndTests`, `PickupClientV4AckOneWayTests`, `PickupClientV4AckOneWayChannelTests`, `PickupClientAckWithoutStatusTests`, `PickupClientAckThreadingTests`); the channel's post-send answer window, a send that does not complete being a `TimeoutException` (`MediatorClientChannelAnswerWaitTests`). Closing right after (#100, PR #143 review round 3; OD-7 and OD-8), over a real Kestrel WebSocket with the mediator's handling held: a 4.0 `AckAsync`, and a single `FetchAsync`, each followed by an immediate dispose leave the mailbox empty when dispose returns, and an `AckAsync` whose connection is then dropped without a close frame leaves it empty soon after (`WebSocketAcknowledgementMailboxTests`). |
| FR-PICK-12 | SHOULD | **Message Pickup 4.0 follow-up.** Also implement `https://didcomm.org/message-pickup/4.0` (renamed protocol; coexists with 3.0 and is selected per session by what the recipient last spoke). Differences from 3.0: `delivery-request.limit` → `message_count_limit`, and the attached count MUST NOT exceed it; times in UTC epoch **milliseconds**; `longest_waited_seconds` removed; empty queue → a `delivery` with an empty `attachments` array (not `status`); `status` carries `thid` = the `status-request` id (REQUIRED in 4.0; this implementation already writes it under 3.0, FR-PICK-02); `delivery.thid` is optional (absent for live deliveries); `messages-received` no longer carries `return_route`; no `status` is sent in reply to `messages-received` under 4.0 (the 3.0 SHOULD is gone and the 4.0 request carries no `return_route`); live mode MUST use `delivery` + `messages-received` and an unacknowledged message MUST be re-queued (FR-PICK-08 made normative); problem-report type `https://didcomm.org/message-pickup/4.0/problem-report` with the same code and comment. The DIDComm v1 form is out of scope (§2.2). **As built (#100):** **Mediator side:** `MessagePickup40Handler` (public, `DidComm.Mediator.Pickup`, constructor `(MessagePickupQueue, ILogger?)`) over the same `MessagePickupQueue` as the 3.0 handler, so a recipient has one mailbox whichever version it speaks — a lease taken through one version is acknowledged through the other, and a session's live pushes take the framing of its last `live-delivery-change` that turned live delivery on. A `status` always carries the request's thread (DD-11) and milliseconds; when nothing can be delivered (empty queue, `message_count_limit: 0`, a `recipient_did` the peer has not registered, only leased, not-yet-due or passed-over messages) the answer is a `delivery` with `"attachments": []` on the request's thread, repeating the filter; a `delivery-request` without a return-route session is not answered and nothing is read or leased (our choice: 4.0 asks for `return_route: all` on every request, and an empty `delivery` sent another way would claim nothing is waiting); `messages-received` is applied and never answered, with or without `return_route`; a request carrying only 3.0's `limit` is malformed. A live push left unacknowledged (its session closed or its lease ran out) goes back to the queue (L308) and is delivered only by a `delivery-request` from then on, never pushed live again, in both versions (owner decision 2026-11-20, "Never push it live again"; L304; FR-PICK-06). `AddMessagePickup()` / `AddMediator()` register it unless `MessagePickupOptions.EnableV4` (default `true`) is `false`; like `EnableV2` (FR-MED-10) it is read when the call runs, and a value in effect that disagrees fails with `InvalidOperationException` when either pickup handler is built. There is no switch to serve 4.0 only; a host can register the 4.0 handler by hand over a `MessagePickupQueue`. The 4.0 codec (`MessagePickup40`, `DidComm.Mediator.Client`) is internal; 4.0 adds no public message API. **Recipient side:** FR-PICK-11. **Spec readings** (4.0 at `decentralized-identity/didcomm.org@a26926c`): a `delivery` answering a `delivery-request` carries `thid` (L34 and L263 read with v2.1 threading.md L17); 4.0's live-mode text adds no push of a delayed message when it falls due (L85, L302–L308; owner decision D2 of 2026-11-14 stands); `messages-received` is not one of the three requests that need `return_route` (L35, L59–L63, L69). | 4.0 fixtures byte-exact; empty queue → empty `delivery` (4.0) vs `status` (3.0) on one store; a 3.0 and a 4.0 recipient on one mediator interleave without cross-talk; both PIURIs disclosed. As built (#100): the page's v2 examples, normalized, round-trip byte-exact, with an empty delivery, a live delivery and a minimal status (`MessagePickup40WireFixtureTests`, `MessagePickup40WireCodecTests`); the handler's decision table (`MessagePickup40HandlerTests`); one store served through both versions, a 3.0 and a 4.0 recipient interleaving, a version switch between requests (`MessagePickupCrossVersionTests`, `PickupV4MailboxEndToEndTests`); registration and disclosure (`MessagePickup40DiTests`); a returned live push is not pushed again, in 3.0 and 4.0 (`PickupLiveNoRepushTests`); the live leg's R-2 cell negotiates 4.0 with this library's mediator, with a negative-control twin. |

---

## 9. Functional Requirements — Transports

(§Transports)

### 9.1 Common

| ID | Keyword | Requirement | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|
| FR-TRN-01 | MUST | Define `IDidCommTransport` (scheme, send, can-handle) and a router selecting transport by `serviceEndpoint.uri` scheme. | Router dispatches http(s)/ws(s). |
| FR-TRN-02 | MUST | Transports carry the IANA media type of the content (e.g. via `Content-Type`). | Correct media type set per envelope. |
| FR-TRN-03 | MUST | Transports are delivery-only — no message effects/results return on the same channel — **unless the return-route extension (§8a) is in effect for a peer authenticated by an authcrypt layer (FR-RR-03)**, in which case replies flow back exactly as FR-RR-04/05 state. A sender MUST NOT assume a synchronous business response otherwise. | No synchronous business response assumed without `return_route`; with `return_route: all` from an authcrypt sender (FR-RR-03) the §8a behavior applies; from a signed-only or `anoncrypt(sign)` sender it does not. |
| FR-TRN-13 | MUST | **Outbound connections are direct and checked; a proxy only when the host names one.** A DID document's `serviceEndpoint` is written by whoever controls that DID, so the HTTP and WebSocket senders the library builds check each connection against `OutboundEndpointPolicy` when they open it (the first, each followed 307, every WebSocket connect and reconnect) and connect straight to the destination. They never use a proxy from the environment (`HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY`, `HttpClient.DefaultProxy`, the system proxy): through a proxy, the check would see the proxy's address, not the destination's. A host MAY name a proxy it trusts (`TrustedProxy`, a `WebProxy`, on `HttpTransportOptions` and `WebSocketTransportOptions`, same semantics on both). The connection to that proxy is not checked against the policy (the host's explicit choice, on any network). The transport then checks every destination itself before handing the send on (each send and each followed 307): the scheme, an IP-literal destination (read as sent on the wire, after IDNA mapping), and, with `ResolveDnsNames`, a best-effort local lookup; a refusal is never retried. Past the proxy the library cannot see where a connection ends up, so **the host's proxy owns egress filtering and MUST refuse internal destinations**; where only the proxy can resolve names, the host sets `ResolveDnsNames = false` and names are the proxy's to filter. A host-supplied connection path (a WebSocket `Connect` delegate, a replaced HTTP primary handler) decides how and where it connects; with `TrustedProxy` set, the destination check still runs before it. **Where the HTTP guarantees come from** (owner decision OD-5 on #110 (2026-11-22): "Docs fix (Recommended)"): following only 307, checking every connection's address and never using the environment's proxy come from the `"didcomm"` client that `UseHttpTransport()` configures; an `HttpDidCommTransport` built by hand with its public constructor gets whatever its client does (over `AddHttpClient()`'s default handler, 301, 302 and 308 are followed and no connection's address is checked). The transport's XML docs say so. | With no `TrustedProxy`, a stand-in proxy set through `HttpClient.DefaultProxy` or any of the three variables receives no connection over http, https, ws or wss, and the guard refuses a private target itself; with one, traffic reaches the named proxy even on loopback under the default policy, and a private or metadata destination (literal, look-alike or after a 307) is refused before the proxy is asked, in one attempt. |

### 9.2 HTTPS (§HTTPS)

| ID | Keyword | Requirement | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|
| FR-TRN-04 | MUST | Send via HTTPS POST with `Content-Type` = the message's media type. | POST issued with correct header. |
| FR-TRN-05 | MUST | Treat any 2xx as success (202 recommended). | 202 and 200 both succeed. |
| FR-TRN-06 | SHOULD | Follow only temporary (307) redirects; do not follow 301/308. This holds for the `"didcomm"` client `UseHttpTransport()` configures (no automatic redirects; the transport follows 307 itself); an `HttpDidCommTransport` built by hand over another client gets whatever that client does (owner decision OD-5 on #110 (2026-11-22): "Docs fix (Recommended)"; FR-TRN-13). **The mediator's direct push** (FR-PICK-09 step 3, #110) follows a `307` the same way, up to the transport's `MaxRedirectHops` (default 5), every new address through the outbound guard, and never a `301`/`302`/`303`/`308`; the whole chain is the message's one attempt, with no retry, and anything but a `2xx` at its end leaves the message for pickup (owner decision OD-1 for #110, 2026-11-22: "Follow 307, tightly bounded"). | 307 followed; 308 surfaced. Direct push: N `307`s make at most `MaxRedirectHops` + 1 POSTs; a loop ends at the bound and a `307` to a guarded address is refused, each leaving the message for pickup; `301`/`302`/`303`/`308` are one POST, not followed; a `307` without a `Location` is one POST, not delivered; a `307` followed to a `2xx` is delivered; through a `TrustedProxy` a private endpoint, and a `307` to a private address, are refused before the proxy is asked (`DirectPushEgressTests`). |
| FR-TRN-07 | MUST | Provide an ASP.NET Core receive endpoint (`MapDidCommEndpoint`) that validates `Content-Type`, unpacks, dispatches to a protocol handler, and returns 202 (or 2xx). Enforce `max_receive_bytes`→413 (FR-API-06). A body that stops arriving before its end (the client went away mid-body, a reset, the server's own body limits) is never unpacked or dispatched and writes one Debug line naming the exception type: a body the server refused is answered with the server's status for it (400 ended early, 408 under Kestrel's minimum data rate, 413 over its `MaxRequestBodySize`), a body whose stream failed (a reset) ends the request, a cancellation that is not the request's abort is answered 400, and a cancellation while the request is aborted propagates; none escapes to the server as an unhandled exception. The spec's HTTPS rules say only that a successful receipt is answered 2xx (transports.md L67), so this refuses no message. A forward re-wrapped for this agent is opened and the message inside is what is dispatched (FR-ROUTE-06, receiver side at a plain endpoint; #110, owner decision OD-3); a message opened out of layers must also be in an envelope the endpoint accepts (`DidCommReceiveOptions.AcceptedMediaTypes`), judged by its own outermost envelope, not the wrapper's, or it is refused like a layer that cannot be opened (#110, O-3). The received body is classified structurally before unpack; the declared media type must be its envelope's (HTTP 415; a STOMP `SEND` is discarded); a bare WebSocket message's envelope type must be in `AcceptedMediaTypes` (discarded otherwise); a body that cannot be classified is refused by the unpack as before (transports.md L66, L79; #110). | TestServer round-trip returns 202; a body that fails mid-read on each HTTP overload is answered with the server's status, 400 for a cancelled read, or ends the request for a failed stream, with nothing dispatched and nothing escaping to the server; on Kestrel a stalled `Expect: 100-continue` body is answered 408 and a body over `MaxRequestBodySize` 413, with no Error line, and a reset mid-body logs no Error (`HttpRequestBodyReadFailureTests`). A message re-wrapped for this agent reaches the consumer on every plain path (`RewrappedReceiveTests`), and one in an envelope the endpoint does not accept is refused (`RewrappedReceiveMediaTypeTests`). A body whose envelope does not match its declared media type is refused `415` over HTTP (the mediator's endpoint included), a STOMP `SEND` whose envelope does not match its `content-type` is discarded, a bare WebSocket message whose envelope type is not accepted is discarded (the mediator's endpoint included), and a body that cannot be classified reaches the unpack refusal as before (`ReceiveDeclaredMediaTypeTests`). |
| FR-TRN-08 | SHOULD | Add resilience (retry w/ backoff, circuit breaker, timeout) on send. **Not for the mediator's direct push** (FR-PICK-09 step 3, #110): it sends through its own instance of the HTTP transport with no retry and a circuit breaker that never opens, so a failing recipient endpoint can neither multiply one message's POSTs nor open a breaker for other recipients or for the host's own sends; its time bound is the push's `PushTimeout`. A failed push is not retried: the message stays in the mailbox and pickup is the later delivery (owner decision OD-2 for #110, 2026-11-22: "Yes, pickup is later delivery"; routing.md L173). | Retries on 5xx/timeout; breaker opens on sustained failure. Direct push: a `503` is one POST whatever the transport's retry setting, a connection failure or a timeout is one attempt, and a failing endpoint never opens a breaker for other recipients or for the host's own sends (`DirectPushEgressTests`); with a process proxy set, the push goes direct (`DirectPushProcessProxyTests`). |

### 9.3 WebSocket (§WebSockets)

| ID | Keyword | Requirement | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|
| FR-TRN-09 | MUST | Each DIDComm message is transmitted individually as one unit of encryption/signing; trust derives from the envelope, not the socket connection. One **WebSocket message** carries exactly one packed DIDComm message. (A WebSocket message MAY be split across multiple WebSocket frames at the protocol level; the implementation MUST reassemble before processing.) **Frame type (#98, owner decision O-98-1):** every DIDComm message this library sends over a WebSocket (the client transport's sends, and the server's same-socket replies and pushes, mediator live deliveries included) is a **text** WebSocket message. The spec names no frame type, and some peers read only text frames (the Hyperledger Identus mediator; browser wallets). STOMP frames (FR-TRN-12) stay binary. On receive, a DIDComm message is accepted in either a text or a binary WebSocket message (releases up to 1.5.x send binary). **A payload that is not valid UTF-8 is refused** (#98): a text frame's payload must be UTF-8 (RFC 6455 §5.6) and a receiver must fail the whole connection on one that is not (§8.1), losing what follows; and a DIDComm message is UTF-8 JSON (FR-ROUTE-01). The WebSocket transport refuses such a payload with `TransportException` before any socket is used, on the pooled, return-route session and STOMP paths; a connection already open keeps serving. On receive, a forward re-wrapped for this agent is opened and the message inside is what is handled (FR-ROUTE-06, receiver side at a plain endpoint; #110, owner decision OD-3); one that cannot be opened, or whose own envelope the endpoint does not accept (`AcceptedMediaTypes`, #110 O-3), is discarded like an envelope that does not unpack. The received body is classified structurally before unpack; the declared media type must be its envelope's (HTTP 415; a STOMP `SEND` is discarded); a bare WebSocket message's envelope type must be in `AcceptedMediaTypes` (discarded otherwise); a body that cannot be classified is refused by the unpack as before (transports.md L66, L79; #110). | One WebSocket message yields one packed DIDComm message; a fragmented WebSocket message reassembles and processes correctly. Every DIDComm message sent (client pooled and session sockets, server replies, pushes and mediator live deliveries) is a text message and every STOMP frame a binary one; a DIDComm message received as text or as binary is processed on both the client and the server (`WebSocketFrameTypeTests`). A payload that is not UTF-8 is refused with nothing written, and the next message arrives on the same connection, on the pooled, session and STOMP paths (`NonDidCommPayloadTests`). A re-wrapped message received on either `MapDidCommWebSocket` overload reaches the consumer, and a bad layer anywhere in the chain is discarded (`RewrappedReceiveTests`, `RewrappedReceiveQaTests`). A body whose envelope does not match its declared media type is refused `415` over HTTP (the mediator's endpoint included), a STOMP `SEND` whose envelope does not match its `content-type` is discarded, a bare WebSocket message whose envelope type is not accepted is discarded (the mediator's endpoint included), and a body that cannot be classified reaches the unpack refusal as before (`ReceiveDeclaredMediaTypeTests`). |
| FR-TRN-10 | MUST | Treat receive transports as one-way by default — a protocol reply is not an HTTP response body and no same-WebSocket reply is assumed — **unless the return-route extension (§8a) is in effect**, in which case replies flow back per FR-RR-04/05 under the key-binding rule below (FR-RR-03 is the same rule). Applications normally deliver replies out of band under explicit egress policy. The registry-aware WebSocket endpoint MAY opt into `AllowSameSocketReplies`, the switch for WebSocket return-route sessions (FR-RR-05); this convenience is not general egress and MUST fail closed: a same-socket reply or push is written only on a session opened by an encrypted inbound with an authcrypt layer (an authcrypt sender key id; a verified signature alone does not count) carrying `return_route: all`, and only from the local DID whose key decrypted that inbound to exactly its sender's DID (FR-RR-03). A header-less message on a socket without a session gets no reply. | Default HTTP/WS receive sends no protocol result; with `AllowSameSocketReplies` on, same-socket replies are written only on a session opened by an authcrypted `return_route: all` inbound and only for its key-bound pair; a header-less message on a socket without a session gets no frame; signed-only/`anoncrypt(sign)`/plaintext/anoncrypt/keyless/cross-tenant/fan-out cases emit no frame; with `return_route: all` from an authcrypt sender, HTTP 200 body / WS push per §8a. |
| FR-TRN-11 | SHOULD | Manage connection lifecycle: pooling by endpoint, keep-alive, reconnect with exponential backoff (1s base, 30s cap, 0.5 jitter — DD-05). Expose lifecycle events. **As built (#100, owner decision OD-7, 2026-11-21): dispose waits, bounded, for the peer's close.** `WebSocketDidCommTransport.DisposeAsync` sends its close frame on each open pooled and session socket (after a STOMP `DISCONNECT` under STOMP) and waits for the peer's answering close. All sockets wait at the same time, so a peer that never answers costs one budget in total, however many sockets are open: the close budget, which is `WebSocketTransportOptions.SendTimeout` (30 s by default), or 0, meaning no wait, when that is infinite or not positive. When the budget runs out the socket is aborted, as before, and the documented bound of the whole call, `DrainBudget` + 2 × the close budget, is unchanged. A pooled socket, which has no other reader, is closed with `CloseAsync`: what arrives before the peer's close is read and dropped. A session socket's close uses the session's own receive loop as its only reader (one reader per socket); what arrives meanwhile is dropped, not handed to the inbound consumer, so a Message Pickup 4.0 live push dropped this way is not acknowledged and the mediator delivers it again. Why: this library's mediator answers a close only after it has handled the messages before it, so a one-way message sent just before dispose, such as a 4.0 acknowledgement (FR-PICK-11), is applied before dispose returns. Before, dispose sent its close and dropped the connection at once. A close the peer starts on a session socket is answered exactly once, before the socket is disposed, whichever of the session's loop, a send or dispose reads it first (RFC 6455 §5.5.1; review round 4), and dispose never throws or leaves a socket behind when host code throws (OD-9). | Reconnect after drop; events fire. As built (#100, round 4): each order of a peer's close against dispose and eviction answers once, before dispose, with one `Disconnected` (`WebSocketDisposePeerCloseOrderTests`, `WebSocketDisposePeerCloseRedTeamTests`). As built (#100, OD-7), on real sockets: dispose returns as soon as the peer answers the close; it waits for a silent peer and gives up at the budget; it waits for every peer at once (one budget in total, never one after another); with an infinite `SendTimeout` it waits for nothing; on the pooled socket data the peer sends before its close is read and dropped; under STOMP it sends `DISCONNECT`, then closes and waits (`WebSocketDisposeCloseWaitTests`). A 4.0 `AckAsync` followed at once by disposing the container returns only once the mediator applied the acknowledgement, on the pooled socket and on a session socket, over the TestServer and real Kestrel (`WebSocketDisposeAcknowledgementEndToEndTests`). |
| FR-TRN-12 | MAY | Support `application/didcomm-encrypted+json` content type when using STOMP over WebSocket. The received body is classified structurally before unpack; the declared media type must be its envelope's (HTTP 415; a STOMP `SEND` is discarded); a bare WebSocket message's envelope type must be in `AcceptedMediaTypes` (discarded otherwise); a body that cannot be classified is refused by the unpack as before (transports.md L66, L79; #110). | STOMP framing optional. A body whose envelope does not match its declared media type is refused `415` over HTTP (the mediator's endpoint included), a STOMP `SEND` whose envelope does not match its `content-type` is discarded, a bare WebSocket message whose envelope type is not accepted is discarded (the mediator's endpoint included), and a body that cannot be classified reaches the unpack refusal as before (`ReceiveDeclaredMediaTypeTests`). |
| FR-TRN-14 | MUST | **A time limit for each WebSocket message.** A WebSocket receive loop reads frames until a message is whole, so it MUST bound how long that may take: each message must arrive whole within `MessageReceiveTimeout`. **Server** (`MapDidCommWebSocket`, both overloads, and `MapDidCommMediator`'s WebSocket endpoint): a connection's first message is timed from the moment the endpoint accepted the connection, so a connection that sends nothing is closed too; every later message is timed from its first part to its last frame; once the first message is in, the wait for the next one is never timed, so an idle return-route session or live-delivery socket stays open. **Client** (`WebSocketDidCommTransport`'s return-route session sockets): the client accepts no connections, so only a message that has started is timed, from its first part to its last frame; the wait for the next push is never timed. One timer per message, never per read; it stops when the last frame is read, before the message is processed, so a handler's or the inbound consumer's work is not timed; a last frame that arrives as the time runs out still counts as in time. **On expiry** the loop never cancels the outstanding receive (in .NET a cancelled receive aborts the socket, and no close frame could follow): it sends a close frame with 1008 (policy violation), reason `message receive timeout`, and drops what it read of the message, of which nothing is processed. The server stops the session writer first, so the close never overlaps a send, then waits for the peer's close within the 5 s close grace and aborts the connection if it does not come; the receive buffer goes back to its pool only when no receive can still write into it; each expiry writes one line that carries no wire value, rate-limited per mapped endpoint (the first and every 1000th at Information, the rest at Debug). The client stops routing sends to that socket (a later return-route send opens a fresh one), sends the 1008 under the socket's send gate and keeps reading in discard mode until the peer's close or its backstop (one `SendTimeout`, or 30 s when that is infinite or not positive), as after an oversized message (1009). **The setting**: `MessageReceiveTimeout` on `DidCommReceiveOptions` (the endpoints) and on `WebSocketTransportOptions` (the client transport), same name and meaning; default 60 s (above the client transport's 30 s `SendTimeout`); `Timeout.InfiniteTimeSpan` turns it off and no timer is created; any other value must be greater than zero and at most `int.MaxValue` ms, else the setter throws `ArgumentOutOfRangeException` (the `HttpClient.Timeout` rule), so a bad value fails where it is set (through `Configure`, when the options are first built), never as a closed connection; timed on the container's `TimeProvider` (the system clock when none is registered; `UseWebSocketTransport` hands the container's to the transport), and a clock that cannot create the timer ends the connection rather than leaving it untimed. The transport reads its value once, when it is built. The mediator endpoint copies the host's value (`ForMediatorEndpoint`). The close handshakes on these endpoints that had no bound — the callback overload's 1009 close and its answer to a peer's close, and the STOMP session's closes (protocol errors and `DISCONNECT`) — are bounded by the same 5 s grace, then aborted. (Spec position: the v2.1 WebSockets section, transports.md, says nothing about timeouts, idle connections or keep-alive, so this limit is local policy like `max_receive_bytes`; it refuses no message a peer finished in time.) **As built (#100, owner decision OD-8, 2026-11-21): a message received in full is finished after its connection ends.** In the loop behind the registry-aware `MapDidCommWebSocket(pattern)` and `MapDidCommMediator`'s WebSocket endpoint, once a message's last frame is read, everything done for it (its STOMP frame, unpack, admission, dispatch and reply) runs under a handling token of its own, not the connection's. While the connection lives nothing cancels that token, so handlers get no new time limit. When the connection's token is cancelled (the peer aborted, the session's writer found the socket broken, or the eviction backstop ran out), a bound starts: the message in hand is still handled for at most the close grace (`WebSocketReturnRouteSession.DefaultCloseGrace`, 5 s, on the container's `TimeProvider`), and only until the host starts to stop (`IHostApplicationLifetime.ApplicationStopping`), whichever comes first. The handling token is also cancelled when the connection's loop ends, so work a handler left running with it is cancelled then. Reads keep the connection's token, so nothing more is read after the drop: a connection has at most one such finish. The return-route session closes at the drop, and a connection that has already ended opens none (FR-RR-05). So a one-way message whose sender goes away right behind it, such as a Message Pickup 4.0 `messages-received` (FR-PICK-11), is still applied. Not covered: the HTTP endpoints, whose acknowledgement gate is deliberate (FR-ROUTE-11, #90: a forward whose request is aborted before its `202` is committed is never relayed), and the callback overload `MapDidCommWebSocket(pattern, onReceive)`, whose token still means the request's abort. What a disconnecting peer can still cause: one bounded finish (at most 5 s) per connection; an evicted connection with a message in hand can live up to the 5 s backstop plus the 5 s finish. **Found while building OD-8:** in the same loop, a handler that throws a cancellation of its own (its own timeout, such as a slow DID lookup or HTTP call) no longer ends the connection: the message is dropped with one Warning line and the connection serves the next message. A cancellation from the connection or the host still ends the loop. | On real Kestrel with a manual clock, over the registry-aware, callback and mediator endpoints (`WebSocketReceiveDeadlineTests`): a silent connection is closed with 1008 when the limit counted from the accept runs out, and not before; a first message finished just inside the limit is dispatched and the connection then stays open, untimed, for a later message; connections that each hold an almost-full first message unfinished are all closed at the limit, and only the first line is loud; a later message stalled after its first part, and one trickled in small fragments with pings between, are closed at the limit counted from that part; an idle return-route session keeps getting pushes past many limits and a stalled message on its socket ends the session with 1008; an idle live-delivery socket on the mediator endpoint stays open and gets the next arrival; under STOMP a connection that never sends `CONNECT` is closed with 1008, and one idle after `CONNECT` stays open and its next `SEND` is dispatched. A 1008, a 1009 and a STOMP protocol-error close that the peer never answers end the connection within the close grace; a close the peer answers completes without an abort; the buffer is not returned to its pool while a receive into it never completes, and is once it has (`WebSocketReceiveDeadlineCloseTests`). The default is 60 s and applies with nothing configured; a value greater than zero and at most `int.MaxValue` ms, or infinite, is kept and any other is refused when set, also through `Configure`; infinite creates no timer, so a silent connection and a fragmented message are untimed; without a `TimeProvider` in the container the system clock times the limit (`WebSocketReceiveDeadlineSettingsTests`); `ForMediatorEndpoint` copies the value (`MediatorWebSocketCoreTests`). Client, against a raw-frame Kestrel peer (`WebSocketSessionReceiveDeadlineTests`): an idle session socket is never timed and a later push reaches the consumer; a stalled push is closed with 1008 at the limit and the rest of the socket is discarded, and a peer that never answers that close is stopped at the backstop; a trickle with pings between its parts is still cut at the limit of its first part; a part already waiting when the time runs out cuts the message unless it is the last; with `ReceiveInbound` off a stalled push is still closed with 1008; a push finished just inside the limit reaches the consumer and a slow consumer is not cut off; a clock that cannot time a message ends the session socket; infinite starts no timer; the default, the setter's rule, the value read once and `UseWebSocketTransport` timing on the container's clock. As built (#100, OD-8), on real Kestrel with a manual clock (`WebSocketMessageFinishTests`): a `messages-received` whose sender aborts the connection while it is being applied, or whose last frame was read just before the connection ended, is still applied; one of which only the first fragment arrived is never applied; after an abort, and after the eviction backstop, the message in hand is applied and the message behind it is never read; the host's shutdown ends a finish before its bound, and the close grace ends a finish that outlasts it, not a tick before; under STOMP a complete `SEND` whose sender aborts is still dispatched; over HTTP a client that aborts its request still cancels the handling; a handler that throws the cancellation of its own timeout leaves the connection serving the next message. |
